People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It feels like if I sit down too long, everything might pile up or fall apart.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Interrupting_Intruding\nmanifestation: \"I interrupt people more than I mean to, especially in committee meetings, because a thought pops into my head and I blurt it out before I lose it, even when someone else is still talking.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I mean, I worry some like anyone, but I can’t say I often feel like something bad is about to happen when things are going fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m usually anxious for no reason… maybe a little unsettled sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with restlessness and settling down caused problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like to admit it, but my family’s had to help me with bills and paperwork. something else.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: Most days I move from the garden to the senior center I don’t think I’m usually anxious for no reason… maybe a little unsettled sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with restlessness and settling down caused problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like to admit it, but my family’s had to help me with bills and paperwork. And with church and the garden, sometimes people have had to change plans or cover things I said I’d do and didn’t follow through on. It’s embarrassing… like they can’t really count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried for more than a few days at a time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for days like that.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I just can’t seem to settle. Like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and should be sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over, or feeling like you need to do certain things in a specific way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in that way, I don’t think. I haven’t noticed needing to do things a certain way like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure. I worry some, like anyone, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. I just feel like I can’t settle, like I’m always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m exhausted and should be sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I suppose I can get short sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of myself as irritable, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I talk more than other people, but… I may not notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself engaging in more activities than others or taking on more tasks than you can handle?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. Most days I’m going from the garden to the senior center, then some church errand, then back home sorting papers or things like that. I don’t really stop much. It feels like if I sit down too long, everything might pile up or fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, more than I’d like. In meetings especially, if something comes into my head, I just say it before I lose it… even if someone else is still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations or have trouble letting others finish speaking when you're in a group?\n\nPatient: Yes… I can, especially in committee meetings. I don’t mean to take over, but if a thought comes to me, I’ll blurt it out before I forget, even if someone else is still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Little things, mostly, but they turn into problems. Like I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or leave someone’s name off the church supper list even after I thought I checked it. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in family gatherings?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Family gatherings can be noisy, and I don’t pay close attention to who’s talking more. I don’t think I’m always the one, but… I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do worry about things, but… overwhelmed? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought about it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like something bad is going to happen, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess. I mean, I worry some like anyone, but I can’t say I often feel like something bad is about to happen when things are going fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m usually anxious for no reason… maybe a little unsettled sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with restlessness and settling down caused problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like to admit it, but my family’s had to help me with bills and paperwork. And with church and the garden, sometimes people have had to change plans or cover things I said I’d do and didn’t follow through on. It’s embarrassing… like they can’t really count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried for more than a few days at a time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for days like that. I get worried here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks, before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I remember even in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but careless. I’d lose papers, talk when I shouldn’t, and they were always reminding me to stay in my seat or finish my work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m worn out and should be sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over, or feeling like you need to do certain things in a specific way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in that way, I don’t think. I haven’t noticed needing to do things a certain way like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not sure. I worry some, like anyone, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. I just feel like I can’t settle, like I’m always needing to get up and do the next thing, even when I’m exhausted and should be sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know. I suppose I can get short sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of myself as irritable, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I talk more than other people, but… I may not notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself engaging in more activities than others or taking on more tasks than you can handle?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. Most days I’m going from the garden to the senior center, then some church errand, then back home sorting papers or things like that. I don’t really stop much. It feels like if I sit down too long, everything might pile up or fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, more than I’d like. In meetings especially, if something comes into my head, I just say it before I lose it… even if someone else is still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often dominate conversations or have trouble letting others finish speaking when you're in a group?\n\nPatient: Yes… I can, especially in committee meetings. I don’t mean to take over, but if a thought comes to me, I’ll blurt it out before I forget, even if someone else is still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Little things, mostly, but they turn into problems. Like I’ll write the wrong amount in the garden checkbook, or leave someone’s name off the church supper list even after I thought I checked it. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in family gatherings?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Family gatherings can be noisy, and I don’t pay close attention to who’s talking more. I don’t think I’m always the one, but… I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel overwhelmed by your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do worry about things, but… overwhelmed? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought about it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like something bad is going to happen, even when things are going well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really, I guess. I mean, I worry some like anyone, but I can’t say I often feel like something bad is about to happen when things are going fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m usually anxious for no reason… maybe a little unsettled sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with restlessness and settling down caused problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like to admit it, but my family’s had to help me with bills and paperwork. And with church and the garden, sometimes people have had to change plans or cover things I said I’d do and didn’t follow through on. It’s embarrassing… like they can’t really count on me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried for more than a few days at a time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for days like that. I get worried here and there, but I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks, before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I remember even in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but careless. I’d lose papers, talk when I shouldn’t, and they were always reminding me to stay in my seat or finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention in class as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did. Teachers would say I was bright, but careless. I was always losing papers, talking when I shouldn’t, and needing reminders to sit still or finish my work.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever diagnosed with attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder as a child?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I was ever diagnosed with that as a child. Not that anyone told me, anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
